ParlaMint-NO_2013-02-12.ana.ud1094e200	I would first like to draw attention to the fact that the statement can extend for a few minutes over an hour.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-02-12.ana.ud1094e205	The regulatory period is up to one hour, but the President will propose, on the basis of the first paragraph of Section 50 of Stortingen’s Rules of Procedure, that the Foreign Minister may exceed the speaking time of one hour by the required number of minutes. – It shall be deemed to have been adopted.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-02-12.ana.ud1094e208	We live in a time of breaking, a time of global change in power and influence. It's about the shift of power between states – from old powers to emerging powers. It is also about the movement of power – from countries to a number of non-governmental actors. The purpose of Norwegian foreign policy is to contribute to stability and predictability in its own neighbourhoods and to ensure Norwegian interests and values in a rapidly changing world. As a small and open economy, Norway is particularly dependent on strong international standards, institutions and driving rules. Binding international cooperation is a prerequisite for fully realising our economic, political and cultural potential. Working for a better organized world is therefore in Norwegian self-interest. We share this idea with many other states. In order to safeguard Norwegian interests in a good way, we must, to the best of our ability, understand the world we are part of and how it evolves. We have to understand what the changes mean for us. Our efforts need to be coordinated and further developed in the light of this. A statement is not a directory of everything we are engaged in. I have chosen to raise some areas that I believe are of particular importance. I would also like to stress that three key issues of Norwegian foreign policy will not be addressed in depth in this statement: EU/EEA issues, UN policy and Norway’s involvement in Afghanistan will be fully addressed in the spring. Traditional power structures are changing. The relative influence of the West is being reduced, not primarily because of economic stagnation in Europe and the United States – although all we have to assume is a passing phenomenon – but because others are growing faster: countries in Asia, in Latin America and increasingly in Africa. It is difficult to fully predict what kind of international order we will face in the years to come. Maybe we'll see something that's completely new, something that can't be described with used terms. New dynamics and new alliances are being created, with which new interdependencies and new contradictions arise. At the same time, traditional power poles and alliances remain both relevant and important. In a less Western-dominated world, therefore, it is at least as important as before that we further develop our European and transatlantic ties. The influence of emerging actors on the world are increasing, but it is still unclear whether they will fill the vacuum of power generated by the weakening of the importance of traditional actors. A new geopolitical picture changes Norway’s space for action. An important objective is to link us more closely to the broader processes that are taking place in Asia, Latin America and Africa. This applies to cooperation with regional forums as well as bilateral contacts with individual countries. All of this is crucial for the design of modern Norwegian foreign policy. Similarly, there is a need to take into account that events that take place far from geographically can have direct consequences for Norwegian interests and values. For this is also a feature of our time: Events far from Norway can hit us harshly and brutally. The attack on the In Amenas gas production plant in Algeria in January was a dramatic reminder of this. Norwegian citizens, Norwegian business and Norwegian interests were directly affected by international terrorism. It also became citizens from many other countries. Explanations of the emergence of international terrorism are many and complex. Terrorism and extremism must therefore be combated by a wide range of instruments, as the Prime Minister explained before 23 January this year. In addition, the terrorist action in Algeria and its handling had many foreign policy dimensions: it was in the consular area — which has always been the basis of the international service — where the Ministry of Foreign Affairs’ crisis management apparatus, along with other Norwegian authorities, Bisto Statoil and the Norwegians who were affected. The terrorist action focused on our increasingly internationalised business and on how the Norwegian authorities can best assist – in safety assessments, in authority contact and in food promotion. Many Norwegian companies — and workers — work in countries where the risk is higher than in Norway. The risk can never be zero. For this reason, together the Norwegian authorities and Norwegian companies must ensure the best possible care for Norwegian interests and citizens. The same applies to a wide range of Norwegian organisations and to the Nordics in international activities. At the same time, we need to be aware that Norwegian companies themselves decide where to establish themselves and that the host country’s authorities are responsible for ensuring the security of international business interests – and others who live in the country. On a deeper level, the terrorist action in Algeria also puts the spotlight on our efforts to combat extremism. This has long been at the heart of Norwegian policy in the Middle East and North Africa, in our engagement policy with new groupings in the wake of the Arab Spring, in our support for vulnerable states with weak state structures, and also in our efforts to promote democracy, human rights and international humanitarian law. In a rapidly changing world, we need to be able to think anew, find new answers, new partners and build new alliances. At the same time, our policies must be firmly anchored in values and principles that are lasting over time. Firstly, it concerns the relationship between countries, which must be based on an international legal order, expressed by international law. Secondly, there are some basic principles on how countries should deal with their citizens. Promoting human rights and democracy is at the heart of government's foreign policy priorities. We therefore advocated a significant increase in measures for a more offensive human rights policy in the year's budget. One of the main objectives is to strengthen freedom of expression and thus the development of open, democratic societies around the world. Freedom of expression is a fundamental human right – a prerequisite for the realisation of other human rights. In concrete terms, Norway supports measures such as the design of democratic media, censorship, promotion of independent media and support for journalistic working conditions in many countries. The government supports Norwegian and international organisations and their local partners working to strengthen freedom of expression and independent media — not least in many Middle East countries. Protection of minorities has been particularly central to the government's work on human rights in the last year. The treatment of minorities is often a clear indication of the functioning of democracy in a society. In our minority project, we have a special focus on beliefs and beliefs that are subject to increasing discrimination and persecution. We continue close and good cooperation with committed and knowledge-rich organisations in Norway – and their contact areas. The situation of minorities of faith and life is regularly raised at political level. We systematically use the UN, the Council of Europe and the OSCE to tackle discrimination and persecution. During the reviews of the UN Human Rights Council, we address the situation of religious minorities. In cooperation with the UN Special Rapporteur on Faith and Life View, we will be organising an international conference in Norway on minorities under pressure this spring. Norway is in dialogue with the Minority Rights Group organisation on a project to strengthen the competence of activists in the Middle East on minority rights. Through the IDEA Democracy Organisation and the Venezia Commission, we support constitutional work focusing on the rights of minorities – again – in the Middle East, and we support actions under the auspices of the Norwegian Helsinki Committee, to mention only something. It is important to say about and protest against human rights violations. We do that, and we're going to do that. But I would warn against an approach to human rights work that settles down on the protest itself, which assumes that as soon as we have raised a matter, we have done ours. Long-term work in this field requires involvement and intervention in the processes and places where the important choices are actually made, such as: in support of constitutional work, security sector reform or strengthening the position of the judiciary in the country of conversion. The size has now to be treated Report. St. 33 for 2011–2012 on Norway and the United Nations. It places great emphasis on the normative role of the UN — particularly in the field of human rights. Norway has good experience of continuing in peace and reconciliation. One example is in Colombia. Colombian government and FARC launched peace negotiations in Norway last October, after six months of exploratory dialogue organised by Norway and Cuba. It is gratifying that the process enjoys broad international support and that the UN plays a role, in particular through consultations with Colombian civil society. Norway, together with Cuba, will continue to assist the parties to the Havana negotiations. At the same time, we must remember that Colombia's government and the FARC bear the primary responsibility for finding a negotiated solution to the conflict, and we must remember that such processes always take time. The exposure in Colombia is a good illustration of Norway’s general approach to peace and reconciliation. We work long-term through diplomatic efforts, support to the UN, to non-governmental organisations and research communities. It is absolutely vital to build trust and trustworthiness. The government will continue to prioritise this work. It is right in a normative perspective – because when we succeed, life and suffering are saved – and Norwegian interests serve to settle conflicts in the world. It is therefore an important foreign policy work in itself, but it is also relevant to show what Norway is doing to other countries, such as Norway. in Latin America – many of whom, like Brazil, are now strengthening their influence globally. Overall, Latin America is experiencing significant economic growth, a better spread of wealth and a reduction in poverty. Among the reasons for this, according to the World Bank, are better education for workers, higher employment and more women entering the labour force. This year, 100 years have passed since women gained general voting rights in Norway, as the third country in the world. Our experience in the field of equality is demand for capital, including in our foreign policy. Internationally, the government strengthens efforts for equality and women's rights through our work in international forums as well as bilaterally through concrete cooperation projects. For many years, Norway has been at the forefront of the global gender equality agenda — now under pressure globally. In particular, there are issues related to sexual and reproductive health. We are therefore stepping up this work in the light of the fact that conservative environments from different communities find each other in the fight against rights we thought we could take for granted in our time. In Pakistan, a brave schoolgirl, the then 14-year-old Malala Yousufzai, was shot and tried to kill. In India, a young woman died of injury after a gang violence. The events upset an entire world. Girls and women are hit by brutal violence. International work on their rights must therefore be strengthened. At the same time, we're going to enjoy people reacting loudly. Again, we see that new social media are being actively used – as an international voice – to channel people's reactions and demand action from political groups. Violence against women is a main theme of the Women's Commission's meeting in New York next month. Norway shall continue to play a driving role here. We also do that with regard to the importance of combating sexualised violence and rape in war. In addition, Norway is the leader in international work to ensure that everyone is entitled to the same human rights regardless of sexual orientation and gender identity. Sexual minorities are often subjected to violence and discrimination. In April, together with the Ministry of Children, Gender Equality and Inclusion, we are organising a major international conference on this theme in Oslo. Norway shall continue to be a driver in the follow-up to United Nations Security Council Resolution 1325 on women, peace and security — and related decisions in NATO, where Norwegian Mari Skare was last designated as the first Special Representative for this field by the Alliance. Norway is working towards a step forward in Resolution 1325 by strengthening effective female participation in conflict prevention and peace-building. The Arab Spring is an example of one of the deepest and most fundamental upheavals of our time. In many ways we're talking about a revolution. As with all the other revolutions, it will take many years for us to be able to fully establish our status. Again, we see that power is being shifted, and that old patterns are being challenged – not only in politics and governance, but also cultural and social, and between generations. Autocratic family dynasties in the Middle East are falling. In some countries, the first steps towards participation and democracy are taken. Elsewhere we see that the road is extremely thorny. In Syria, it goes from bad to worse. What started out as peaceful protests against a brutal regime has today been exasperated by a bloody civil war. Despite strong regional involvement, the international community has not managed to gather around joint efforts to put an end to violence. The situation is deeply tragic, and there are few signs of a quick solution. The UN estimates that over 60,000 people are killed, four million need emergency aid, more than 700,000 have fled, and over two million are internally displaced. The outlook for improving the situation of the civilian population is low as long as the conflict is ongoing. The regime's violent abuse of power against the population is increasing. The combatants are in violation of basic humanitarian principles and rules of warfare on a daily basis. The population and humanitarian aid personnel are exposed to calculated attacks. It makes the work of helping the affected inside Syria very difficult. It is essential that Norway and the international community continue to put pressure on humanitarian access and protection partners in all parts of Syria. Norway is also among the largest humanitarian contributors. From the Norwegian side, it is a priority task to prevent the regional spread of the conflict. Our assistance, not least for Lebanon's handling of Syrian refugees, is designed to prevent the presence of refugees from triggering latent conflicts and thus destabilising the country. Norway's clear position is that President Assad has lost all legitimacy and must leave. In today’s situation, Norway considers the National Coalition to be the legitimate representative of the Syrian people. Norway supports the Syrian opposition forces working together within the national coalition, politically and through cooperation projects. Respect for humanitarian principles, human rights and minority rights is as clear for the opposition as for the regime forces. Although the coalition was a progress, it's a long way to go until we have a complete and united opposition with the ability to take over power and secure a stable and orderly way out of chaos. There are also a number of actors, inside and outside Syria, who take advantage of the situation to promote objectives that are not in the long-term interest of the Syrian people. Norway supports the mission of the United Nations and the Arab League, Lakhdar Brahimi, in his efforts to seek a basis for a political solution. But more than anything else, it is the special responsibility of the members of the UN Security Council to bring to an end the civil war and the humanitarian suffering in Syria. It is important to remind the Security Council that their responsibility for international peace and security is a responsibility they manage on behalf of all UN Member States. It's time for the Security Council to take that responsibility. It is also important that concrete plans are now made for what we do on the day the conflict itself is over, given both the extent of the damage and the strong internal tensions that the conflict has intensified. A particular concern is related to what happens to the assumed large stocks of chemical weapons in Syria. For Egypt, last year was turbulent, and the unrest continues. The June presidential election, the dissolution of Parliament and the constitutional process, combined with economically demanding times, have created discontent and frustration and have brought many Egyptians back into the streets. Norway has a close dialogue with the Egyptian authorities. Norwegian aid has escalated and is mainly intended to strengthen the development of democracy: support for free press, civil society and to safeguard women's rights. We have made it clear that all Egyptians must have the same rights, regardless of gender or religious affiliation – including the Coptic population. We encourage all parties in Egypt to moderate. The close contact we have established over several years, not least with the Muslim Brotherhood, enables us to take up with weight the problematic and worrying features of the development of today's rulers in Cairo. The Egyptian government played a central role in bringing Israel and Hamas to a truce in Gaza last year. Egypt’s willingness and ability to play a positive role in the region is encouraging. On 29 November last year, as you know, Palestine was granted a large majority of observer status in the UN General Assembly. On the basis of the resolution, the government has decided that we are now talking about what we have previously called the Palestinian Territory, such as Palestine. As head of the donor country group for Palestine, AHLC, we are following developments in the Middle East particularly closely. The next meeting will be held in Brussels in March, and it will be important. The economy of the Palestinian authorities is in deep crisis. The donor contribution last year was lower than expected and contributed to an increase in the debt burden of the Palestinians. We notice a dawning yield fatigue in the international community. Large resources and a lot of work are devoted to building up a manageable Palestinian authority. As the leader of the AHLC, Norway is working to secure the resources necessary to prevent economic collapse. The truce in Gaza is still fragile. At the same time, there seems to be a growing recognition, including in Israel, that an improved security situation in and around Gaza has a clear link with economic development. Armistice, Israeli relief in the decommissioning regime and expansion of the fishing zone are good, but more is needed to achieve economic stability and growth. At the same time, Israel continues the development of illegal settlements on occupied land. They're international lawless, they're undermining the possibility of peace and they're threatening the entire two-state solution. After a time when the peace process has been completely locked down, after elections and government formations in the United States and Israel, new attempts will now be made to revive the process. Norway will actively support this. A prerequisite for success is that the Palestinian Authority continues to function. As a result of the major upheavals in the region, Norway’s involvement in northern Africa has been strengthened. Developments in several countries are now at a crucial stage, with the creation of new constitutions that will provide the basis for more democratic political systems. In order to succeed in this fragile situation, both authorities and civil society need external support, in particular for empowerment measures. The emergence of moderate political movements, with or without anchoring in Islamic faith, is an important part of the picture. In several countries, we also see a emergence of radical political Islamism, and an increasing number of young people are attracted to militant groupings. Together with other countries, we will continue to support vulnerable migration processes in North African countries to ensure democratisation, fundamental rights and better living conditions in the long term. We are now strengthening contact with the countries of the region, including with a view to addressing security challenges. The Sahel countries have long been marked by poverty, political unrest, porous borders and organised crime in the form of trafficking in human beings and drug trafficking, arms and goods. The region is also severely affected by climate-induced food crises. The collapse of the Gaddafi regime in Libya also appears to have contributed to the crisis in Mali. Lead soldiers brought weapons, returned home, and fed the long-standing conflict between the peoples of northern Mali and the government of Bamako. In March 2012, Northern Mali came under the control of Islamist insurgents with connections to Al Qaida and organized crime. The insurrection in turn triggered a military coup in Bamako that threw the country into a political crisis. This further weakens the country’s ability to address the safety challenges in the north. North Mali could be developed as a haven for international terrorist networks with foregleams to Al Qaida, North Africa, and other groups in Africa that threaten regional and western interests – i.e. a development against serious cross-border security threats that the world community is difficult to deal with. An international African force, AFISMA, was mandated by the United Nations Security Council on 20 December 2012 by Resolution 2085 to launch a military intervention to assist the Malian authorities in regaining control throughout their territory. On 11 January, France intervened against an immediate threat from the Islamic uprisings in Northern Malta. The operation took place at the request of the President of Mali and was supported by the UN Security Council and many African countries. Algeria authorised the use of its airspace. French efforts are scheduled to be stepped down as soon as African forces are in place and are able to take over management. And this is exactly what's important. I believe that the time of the great Western intervention is over after Iraq and Afghanistan. In a country like Mali, a long-standing and high-profile Western military presence could be counterproductive. You just risk strengthening the enemy images that Al Qaida is trying to build up. Therefore, the solutions must lie in the region. It believes the UN Security Council, it believes the African Union and it believes ECOWAS, the economic community of the West African States. I discussed possible responses to the Mali crisis during my meetings with the African Union in Addis Ababa just before Christmas. However, it may take time for an African force to be able to fight. The safety forces of Mali must be brought into operational condition. A French proposal to turn AFISMA into a peacekeeping UN operation as soon as conditions permit is discussed right now in the UN Security Council. Norway supports such a proposal. In this case, such a UN force will continue to be composed mainly of African components, while opening up to non-African contributions. If such a UN force becomes effective, the government will decide on possible Norwegian participation, and the Stortinget will be consulted in line with usual practice. The EU is working to put in place a training and training team in Mali – EUTM. Norway will give priority to any UN-led operation and thus will not contribute to this EU force. Faced with the challenges in Mali and Sahel, we need a broad strategy with a whole range of instruments – political and economic. Norway supports the efforts of the UN and regional organisations to help local authorities in Mali and the region to combat poverty, strengthen governance and take care of their own security. The government has just decided to add up to $30 million. to the United Nations Fund for Mali, which is additional to the annual Norwegian aid of approximately 80 million. kr to the country. We are continuously assessing further contributions in close dialogue with the authorities, the UN and other donors. Far south on the continent continues the tragedy in the eastern Congo and around the large seas. A human drama has been unfolding for years, largely away from the daily search for light from the world's media and the international political agenda. This is one of the many so-called forgotten conflicts that we, as an international society, must have stronger impetus in our collective consciousness. Eight states in the region have recently reached agreement on a stability plan in the Democratic Republic of Congo that the United Nations has put in place. Norway supports this work. It would be totally wrong, however, if it were now created a notion that Africa is a continent dominated by crises and misery. It's actually longer from the truth today than in a very long time. Overall, Africa has been the second fastest growing part of the world in the last ten years. Of the 20 fastest growing economies in the world today, eleven are African. Growth has been driven by increased political stability and economic reforms. Increased diversity in economic cooperation and trade with several parts of the world, not least with Asian countries, have made several African countries more robust to fluctuations in the world economy. In fact, there is a basis for considerable optimism. Several countries have experienced marked rises in key indicators, such as: Public health. A number of African governments show an enhanced willingness and ability to address their own challenges. More and more people are making democratic elections. Addis Ababa in Ethiopia, where the African Union has its head office, is increasingly seen as Africa's political capital. This also contributes to more effective regional decisions and access to continent decision-makers. In order for growth to be sustainable, it is important that it be invested in and traded with African countries. Because in the long run, there is little doubt that trade is even more important than assistance. I am pleased to note that the Norwegian business community has discovered the great opportunities Africa holds, and that a number of companies consider increased involvement. Increased macroeconomic stability and a more nutritious policy in many African countries, as well as better education and a high level of labour, are conducive to further growth. Norway has a clear interest in supporting the positive forces that are working to combat poverty and the deep conflicts that still characterise some African countries. Our own continent, Europe, is also experiencing a break-up. Last year's worst predictions for Europe, the euro area and the euro area did not come true. Despite weak economic developments, confidence in the euro has been strengthened in markets, much thanks to untraditional measures taken by the European Central Bank. The EU has taken a number of concrete decisions – such as the repayment of emergency loans to Greece and the development of a banking union for the euro area and other interested EU countries. European leaders have demonstrated their willingness to defend the euro. Borrowing costs for countries such as Spain and Italy are significantly reduced. Long-term reform programmes are implemented in all crisis countries. The Council of the European Union has agreed that 11 countries are working on a common tax on financial transactions under the enhanced cooperation procedure. It may also affect Norway. But even if a lot goes in the right direction, the crisis has set very deep tracks. Long-term debt building in many countries, macroeconomic imbalances in the euro area and rigidity in the adaptability of the economy will take time to see results. In addition, significant challenges arise as a result of demographic changes in Europe. It is necessary to have a long-term perspective, because in the short term, the real economic outlook is slim. Several countries have zero growth or decline in production. What began as a financial crisis has become a unemployment crisis. The availability of the euro area is increasing, not least among young people. Weak growth, high unemployment and high debt will continue to be experienced by many countries, both within and outside the euro area. In many places, differences are also increasing. The Secretary-General of the OECD, José Ángel Gurría, is one of many who warns against this. Increasing inequality contributes both to political instability and to poor macro-economy. For Norway, developments in Europe are very important. If demand for our goods fails, this has consequences for the economy and employment. Therefore, an economic policy that allows for rapid action and intervention in such a situation is crucial, and there is something the government intends to do. In April Norway is the host of the International Labour Organisation (ILO) European Regional Assembly in Oslo. It is part of the follow-up to the government's strategy for decent work. Norway shall strengthen its efforts for workers’ rights and cooperation with the ILO shall be given a boost. The meeting will bring together 500 participants under the theme ‘Workplaces, Growth and Social Justice’. Norway is also appointed to chair the Ministerial Council meeting of the Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development, OECD, in Paris at the end of May. We will thus also have an influence on this agenda, and we will focus on the subjects I mentioned, on the ILO collection: growth, jobs and social justice. In times of crisis, democracy is put under pressure. The darkest chapters of European history show the link between economic crisis and political instability. At the same time, democracy, human rights and the rule of law are strong in today's Europe. We see, however, that the challenges on our own continent are increasing. Minorities are also put under pressure here. Antisemitism, Islamophobia and various forms of racism have come to the surface more clearly. We also have painful experience here at home through the attack on 22 July 2011. The picture in Europe is complex, but we see extra-right currents intensifying in several places, preferably with a backstop of hateful utterances channelled through online communities and social media. In this form of extremism, excluding nationalism and attacks on minorities are important driving forces. High rectorism leaps out of Europe's majority society and has deep and long roots for political violence on our own continent. We also see other forms of political extremism – such as jihadism – that have destructive power. Norway is part of the European picture. We need more knowledge and broader analysis. We need to follow developments closely. We will take responsibility by contributing to raising the issue on the political agenda in Europe. We have therefore established a concrete project to coordinate and strengthen Norway's contribution to the protection of democracy, minority rights and the fight against right-wing extremism in Europe. Europe’s leading defence against extremism is the binding European cooperation developed after the Second World War — in the EU, in the Council of Europe, in the OSCE and in NATO. The core of this is belief in democracy, the rule of law, fundamental human rights as well as open and tolerant societies. In the times we are going through, the government considers it particularly important to strengthen the defence of these principles – at home, in Europe and globally – and against anyone who wants to undermine them. Norway should lead the fight against increased intolerance, hateful expressions and right-wing extremism in Europe. I will mention two measures in this context: Through the EEA funds, Norway has established a comprehensive framework agreement with the Council of Europe. In cooperation, we look at challenges related to fundamental rights, justice, the protection of minorities and the fight against intolerance and hateful expressions. Through the EEA funds, we are also among the most important contributors to civil society in the central European EU countries. More than 1.1 billion. programmes for voluntary organisations for projects capable of strengthening democratic values and human rights, measures to combat hateful expressions, racism, anti-semitism and homophobia. In Romania and Bulgaria, 10 pst. by the EEA funds are linked to measures taken by the space people. The aim is to improve living conditions, to strengthen integration and to combat discrimination. We also strengthen Norway's cooperation with the Council of Europe and the work on democracy and human rights in the OSCE, which is the forum in which Norwegian parliamentarians also participate. Some countries are in focus. In Ukraine, more developments raise concerns. The imprisonment of former Prime Minister Timoshenko and other former members of the Government gives the impression of selective prosecution. A party with a strong nationalistic agenda has wind in its sails. The parliamentary elections seem to be a step backwards in the country's democratic development. A legislative proposal has been put forward to make the situation of sexual minorities even more difficult. Norway has clearly expressed its concern about all such developments — most recently during the visit of its Prime Minister to Norway last November. The development of Belarus – Europe’s last dictatorship – is discouraging and worrying. Together with the international community, Norway continues to exert pressure. We are in line with the EU sanctions regime, and we are continuing concrete financial support for the promotion of democracy and human rights. In economic development, Azerbaijan has made important progress, but progress is still unevenly distributed, and much remains before it fully fulfils its human rights and democracy obligations. From the Norwegian side, we will continue to communicate our views on developments in the country, and we support measures to contribute to respect for fundamental rights. I'm going to take a long run – to the north! When the government parties presented Soria Moria I in 2005, we decided that the northern regions would be Norway’s main strategic area – not one of several, but the most important. Very much has happened in both Norwegian and international north-eastern policies since then. The northern regions are on the international political map. In the first place, the job focused on raising international interest and attention on the development of the Arctic. In the next round we had probably achieved this, but now it became important to counteract an impression of the Arctic as an area of rivalry and strife. Not long ago you could get this impression through international media. The truth is that we are working systematically to establish a unified understanding of the fundamental rules and institutions that must underpin peaceful, constructive cooperation in the north. We have also succeeded, and there we are today. A good framework is in place. All countries in the region agree to both the establishment of the law of the sea and the strengthening of Arctic Councils — to mention two of the main factors. That is why we are now at the beginning of a new chapter in the north, a chapter that is more than anything else about using the many possibilities we have – in our country as well as in the other countries of the Arctic. Much has happened over these years: we have increased preparedness in northern seas through our own actions and international cooperation. The BarentsWatch marine monitoring system and the search and rescue agreement negotiated following an initiative in the Arctic Council are examples of this. We have strengthened our presence in the north, among others. through the transfer of the Armed Forces Operational Headquarters to Bodø, the establishment of the Coast Guard Headquarters in Blackland and the acquisition of five new frigates and six new coastal corvettes to improve Norway’s capacity in the northern sea areas. Norway currently owns one of Europe's most modern and highly revalued defences, for which we have great recognition from our allies. This is important both for foreign policy in general and for north-eastern policy in particular. We have also worked hard to improve our understanding of developments in the Arctic within NATO. The premise that Northern Region policy should be possible is that through our security policy, we succeeded in creating predictability and clarity in our own neighbourhoods. In addition, we have strengthened and clarified Nordic foreign and security policy cooperation in important areas – such as material cooperation, exercises, air surveillance and digital security. A whole new dynamic has come into Nordic cooperation. Furthermore, we have contributed to a marked rise in environmental and climate knowledge – established in Tromsø, and increased resources for polar and climate research. We have focused on the development of nutrition on land, among others. within the mineral industry and travel. With the Barents Sea demarcation agreement in place, the mapping of petroleum resources on the seabed is now under way. In the fisheries sector, we can refer this year to the largest cod quota in the Barents Sea. Good cooperation with Russia on fisheries management has been important to achieve this. Good locations and good environmental status in the northern areas also provide a suitable starting point for the further development of the aquaculture industry in the north. We have a continuous dialogue with Russia on visa facilitation, more effective border crossing, customs and similar issues. The regime of border residence certificates entered into force on 29 May last year. On the Norwegian side, we want to harmonise our visa procedures with Finland, which will make it easier for Russians to obtain multiple travel visas to Norway. We have strengthened business cooperation with Russia. Our Embassy in Moscow is strengthened with competence in education, research and innovation. Much of this has contributed to a whole new economic dynamics in North Norway. The main challenge today is access to enough skilled labour. We see the growth of a dynamic business in the north that shows the ability and the willingness to assert itself internationally. But our northern vantage point also gives us orchestral space to observe global climate change. Last fall, the new alarming facts came. The prevalence of sea ice in the Arctic Ocean has been at the lowest level since modern measurements started in 1979. The multi-year period melts, the tundra tin, the greenhouse effect amplifies. Climate change must meet with global efforts. We also need to use the cooperation structures in the north to find solutions, both to combat climate change and to adapt to the changes that will have to happen in any case. During our Presidency period in the Barents Council, until October this year, the government will draw up a climate action plan for the Barents region. In addition, economic development, the environment and people-to-people cooperation are the main initiatives for this Presidency and work on a transport plan for the region. The highlight will be the marking of the first 20 years of Barents' cooperation in the Church in June. There we aim to sign a new Church Declaration to complement the 1993 and 2003 Declarations and to provide a framework for further cooperation. I am proud and pleased with what we have achieved in Barents' cooperation during these 20 years. It's been noted internationally. I will give tribute to the active role played by the counties and many municipalities in the north and by depositors. Arctic Councils have become more important in recent years, and we discussed this in an interpellation last month. Right after that, I had the pleasure of signing the host country agreement for the Arctic Council during a ceremony in Tromsø. This confirms the status of the city as the capital of the Arctic. The agreement concluded between the Member States of the Arctic Council on search and rescue will be followed up by an agreement on oil protection, to be signed at the Kiruna ministerial meeting in May. Furthermore, we hope to reach agreement on the inclusion of new permanent observers. Norway supports the admission of new observer countries meeting the criteria adopted at the Nuuk ministerial meeting in 2011. Our attitude to the admission of new permanent observers is known internationally. The same is true of our invitation to the candidate countries to dialogue with Norway on Arctic issues, of course China. The issue of observers illustrates the growing interest in northern areas from outside the region — not least from Asian countries. In line with climate change, the Arctic Ocean is increasingly seen as a sea that binds North America, Europe and East Asia together. Cooperation in the Arctic is taking place within defined frameworks of international law. There is full agreement that the law of the sea identifies the framework for action in the Arctic — in the fields of environmental protection, research, petroleum activities, the establishment of the outer boundary of the continental shelf and shipping. In the future, the government of the North Region will pay particular attention to knowledge building, value creation, technology development and other challenges facing the region. We need to develop the Barents Sea into a new oil and gas region in Europe – in a balanced way with important fisheries, climate and environmental considerations. We need to build a robust and environmentally sound mineral industry – which will facilitate the forthcoming mineral strategy. We will continue to develop the highest standards for petroleum extraction, movement and transport in demanding climatic areas. Today, we have broad cooperation with Russia in many of the areas of society I have just mentioned. A good neighbourly relationship has always been important for Norway. We'll look for new opportunities to further strengthen this. Developments in Russia affect us in many ways, and today it faces a number of challenges. Modernisation and reform are needed to avoid economic and social stagnation, which in turn may lead to increased dissatisfaction among the population. In order to modernise the economy and society, international cooperation, open contact and good framework conditions are essential. It will therefore continue to be important to engage Russia, both bilaterally and internationally. We closely monitor the human rights situation in Russia. The development here gives cause for concern. Human rights are under increased pressure, and this is addressed in our contacts with the Russian authorities. Following the presidential election, several legislative changes have been introduced which limit the scope of civil society and political opposition. This is clearly unfortunate for the development of democracy. We are also concerned that this could go beyond the cooperation between Norwegian and Russian civil society – precisely because this people-to-people cooperation is such an important platform for relations between Norway and Russia. It is therefore important for us that cooperation at grassroots level is not affected. The suspension of the activities of the indigenous organisation RAIPON gave a alarming signal. We've brought this up with the Russian authorities. On several occasions we have expressed concern and concern at the negative developments, bilaterally and in multilateral forums, such as the OSCE and the Council of Europe. The close contact and the broad cooperation we have provide us with a good starting point for addressing also difficult questions. At the same time, we continue our support for project cooperation between Norwegian and Russian civil society and for the work on human rights and non-governmental organisations. The world's energy landscape is undergoing significant changes, changes that will have a major impact on our foreign policy as well. In Asia, demand for energy is growing and contributes to a strong increase in world energy consumption. The United States is experiencing a technological revolution in drilling for shale oil and shale gas, which will also have consequences for Norway. The International Energy Agency, the IEA, tracks that the United States will be the world's largest oil producer in a few years' time, and that North America will be a net energy exporter as early as 2030. The prospect of the US becoming almost independent of energy imports must also be assumed to have extensive geopolitical implications, such as: in the relationship between the United States and the Arab oil producing countries. OPEC is strongly influenced by this. Over 90 pst. As a result, Middle East oil exports are expected to go to Asian countries in 2030. There is every reason to believe that this will help to make emerging powers in Asia more interested in the political development of oil-producing regions. Norway is an energy storm power and is also perceived as such in the world. We're the third largest gas exporter in the world. We account for one third of EU imports of gas. We are Europe's largest renewable energy producer, and our production is going to increase to 67.5 pst. of total energy consumption by 2020. Our hydropower production is the world's sixth largest, in front of countries like India and France. Europe is asking for our clean energy and new opportunities for exchange with our hydropower. The transmission capacity of the network to Europe has increased and will be further increased by 2020 in the framework of security of supply in Norway. An even closer energy cooperation will develop in Europe in the coming years, and Norway will be an important part of this picture. In addition, through Oil for Development, there is a high international interest in the Norwegian model for the management and organisation of oil and gas resources. All in all, our oil and gas interests – together with the increasing international involvement of Norwegian companies – also face new challenges. It becomes an increasingly important task to understand the geopolitical trends that result from a global energy landscape in rapid change and to understand what this means for us. At the same time as Norway has become a power of energy, the internationalisation of other Norwegian businesses has also been reinforced. In the 1990s, the 30 largest Norwegian enterprises had one third of their employees abroad. Already today, the proportion is two-thirds. Norwegian maritime enterprises are present in the most important growth markets in the world. The Norwegian Veritas – to take just one example – has 300 offices in 100 countries. The marine industry produces 1 million. tonnes of salmon each year exported to the world. Telenor is the world's eighth largest mobile operator. We have built up the world's largest state investment fund, the state pension fund abroad, with investments in well over 7,000 companies around the world. We benefit from globalisation and its specialisation. However, our economy, our well-being and our employment depend very much on global economic development. Our dependence on the petroleum sector means that we are vulnerable. We depend on having a business that creates values and that is and remains competitive in an international market. In order to ensure future well-being and value creation, we must continue to exploit and promote our advantages in different areas also internationally. We must support the development of the sectors that are most at international level. We also need to promote a wider range of businesses, products and services. Major investments and important Norwegian interests in countries and regions that have not previously had much presence from Norwegian business also require news of how security, preparedness and crisis management must be met. All of this I have pointed out about the very internationalisation of Norwegian business, is part of the background that the government is now strengthening its efforts to promote Norwegian business. Economic diplomacy shall be given a higher priority. We look specifically at how we can adapt the EEAS to better serve Norwegian business interests abroad. This includes the organisation of work, the tools and instruments we use, the partners and interlocutors we choose abroad, and how we can best assist in countries where there is little political and economic predictability and lack of legal certainty. My position is that it is never wrong for Norwegian businesses to establish themselves in any country, with the exception of places where this would be contrary to international sanctions regimes. My experience is that the presence of Norwegian industry contributes rather positively than negatively — at least as long as they are involved in social responsibility, promotes decent work and combats corruption. In addition, the government wants to achieve greater synergies between development assistance and Norwegian business, and we look at how international research cooperation can be used more strategically to promote Norwegian business interests abroad. In a short time, we will present a notice on Norway’s place in global cultural cooperation. Together with the business community, we will continue our efforts to promote reputation and social responsibility. The Minister of Industry and Trade and I also look at how we can strengthen cooperation with other departments and their instruments. We have launched a broad and inclusive process involving business. We're going to act together and co-ordinated out there. Therefore, in close cooperation with the Minister of Industry and Trade, I want to focus more on our Team Norway model. Here we can be inspired by our Nordic neighbour, who has chosen to bring together all the actors involved in the international marketing of Finland and Finnish companies in their Team Finland, and we can learn from the organisation of this. We find large parts of the new markets in Asia. Norwegian value creation is directly affected by developments in this part of the world. We see an Asian counterpart to the growth and market development that in its time made Europe and the United States the main trading partners of Norway. The fact that Norway has come out better than many other European countries in today’s economic situation is largely due to the increasing demand for many of the goods and services exported by Asian countries, while demand for the same goods and services has fallen in more traditional markets. Asia today accounts for well over half the world’s population and an increasing share of the world’s gross domestic product. But Asia is far from just a growing business arena for the interests of Norwegian or other countries. Asia's emergence is about politics, technology, research, culture and much more. In our part of the world, more and more people look east. In the United States too, there is a growing trend towards Asia, both politically and economically. In the 21st century, the United States will appear to be as much a Pacific power as an Atlantic power. The relationship between the United States and China thus becomes one of the most important bilateral relations of our time. The increasing tension we see in the South China Seas poses a new challenge. This is important for us to understand, also as close allies of the United States. We condemn the night's bombing in North Korea for the most part. It is a flagrant violation of the standards of the Test stop agreement and represents a serious threat to international peace and security. The world community must be united in its response to the North Korea nuclear programme. Strong economic growth in Asia has increased optimism and political confidence in many countries in the region. Increased trade and investment links Norwegian technology, competence and capital directly to Asian markets and industrial and knowledge environments. At the same time, we must be aware of the many challenges we face in several countries: high growth rates, partly weak economic structures, cultural and political inequalities between countries, poor commercial policy frameworks, the risk of corruption and increasing disparities in living conditions and income in several countries. The region as a whole is still characterised by varying degrees of democracy, good governance and growing environmental problems. Together with the needs for resources and commodity access for the emerging economies, this gives rise to internal tensions and regional conflicts. From the Norwegian side, we give high priority to understanding the trends and impacts on Norwegian interests. This work will lead to an action plan for our investment in Asia that will focus on how we must seek to exploit the many new opportunities. In order to safeguard Norwegian interests, it is important to think strategically and in the long term towards Asia. We will be closer to the wider processes that are taking place in the region. We see that established regional structures have new and heavier roles and that others are emerging. We're going to make our common interests visible and promote in our meetings with Asian partners. In regional fora and bilaterally, we will continue to have normative issues high on the agenda in our dialogue with Asian countries. One of the most important value debates in our time is the one that takes place within emerging Asia. As in other parts of the world, this debate is also about the relationship between rulers and those who are overthrown, about democracy, human rights, freedom of expression and human dignity. We should highlight the importance of international law for the relationship between land and for security and freedom of movement in all marine areas. Like many of our partners in Asia, Norway has strong maritime traditions. The maritime sector is an anchoring party in our trade with many Asian countries. We're going to meet the growing international interest in the Arctic from central Asian countries with commitment and dialogue, both to better understand their interests and motives and to develop Norwegian views in the north. The exchange of visits is an illustration of how we see – and travel – to the east. Last year the royal house, the prime minister, myself and several other members of the government made a series of extensive visits to Asia. The Crown Princess couple visited Indonesia in November with a large business delegation in energy, maritime trade and infrastructure. The Prime Minister visited South Korea and later Japan with a large business delegation and Myanmar. The Prime Minister and I both participated in the ASEM, Asia–Europe Forum, Laos, where Norway was admitted as a new member. This forum provides us with unique opportunities for high-level political exchange and more informal contacts with Asian partners. Even I visited Laos, Myanmar and Singapore and in November Bali Democracy Forum where a number of Asian leaders participated. This is an important Indonesian initiative to promote democratic processes and human rights in the region. In April, I intend to attend the Community of Democracies’ Ministerial Meeting in Mongolia – another country that, following extensive and positive political reforms, raises the tab of democracy promotion in the region and globally. Norway has also launched a dialogue on a broader partnership with ASEAN, the South-East Asian Cooperation Organisation. This is the main institutional framework for multilateral cooperation in Asia. Norway is in the process of establishing a formalised partnership and has decided to accede to the ASEAN Treaty on Friendly Relations and Cooperation in Southeast Asia during the year. Through our fresh membership of ASEM and our future relationship with ASEAN, we can participate in and influence the regional agenda. It also gives us an opportunity to discuss global issues such as security policy, environment, climate and health – areas where Asian countries are important actors and face particular challenges. Therefore, the government is now strengthening cooperation with the region’s institutions and with individual countries. Some examples: India is a very important player in the new global and regional dynamics. At the bottom is the strategy for cooperation between Norway and India presented by the government in 2009. We have worked systematically for a long time to expand cooperation in policies, economics and business, sustainable development and health. The presence is also strengthened. We have put in place a new tax agreement, and we are now negotiating a trade agreement within the framework of EFTA to ensure that Norwegian businesses get as predictable framework conditions as possible. Norwegian business shows increasing interest in India. Furthermore, in the future, the government will continue its research cooperation with India. Indonesia has undergone an impressive process of democratisation, strong economic growth and is now the largest economy in Southeast Asia. As the world's most populous Muslim country, Indonesia is an important bridge builder in the region, a voice for diversity and tolerance, and a driving force for regional integration and a clearer ASEAN. Norway has established a strategic partnership with Indonesia. We work together in economics and business, climate, forests and energy, promoting human rights and democracy. It is gratifying that last year Innovation Norway opened its office in Jakarta to assist Norwegian business. South Korea is also becoming an increasingly important partner for Norway. During the visit of President Lee to Oslo in September 2012, we concluded two important cooperation agreements in the maritime sector with South Korea. The fourth year was an eventful year in Chinese politics. At the Communist Party Congress, a new generation of leaders came into the top positions. A new president and a new government will be appointed at the People's Congress in March. China’s new leadership faces major tasks related to economic and social change. We look forward to good and constructive cooperation with the new leaders of China. Norway historically has a good relationship with China. We were among the first countries to recognise the People's Republic. Friendship and close relations between our two countries have evolved over a number of years and many visits have taken place. There are considerable opportunities for cooperation in a number of areas, such as: the maritime sector, environment, energy and Arctic. The 2010 Nobel Peace Prize award has damaged the bilateral relationship between Norway and China. In Chinese teams, it was claimed that the allocation was an interference in Chinese internal affairs. The government acknowledges the negative consequences of the situation that has arisen. This is the background for the government's active efforts to put the bilateral relationship back on track. Together with China, we have a common interest in strengthening bilateral relations, based on mutual respect. As part of this work, Prime Minister Stoltenberg had a brief meeting with Prime Minister Wen Jiabao under ASEM in Laos. The government also maintains close contact with Norwegian business operators in China. We appreciate China's understanding and cooperation to normalize our relationships in the dialogue that is taking place. Today's China is a global player both economically and politically. China's choices in areas such as economics, distribution, the development of the rule of law, migration and the environment will have an impact on an entire world. We respect China's right to choose freely its path of development. We look forward to good dialogue with China on issues of common interest in the future. A stable China is in Norway's interest. The switchovers in Myanmar have been nothing less than impressive for the past six and a half years. In two weeks the president of the country, Thein Sein, will visit Norway. Last November, the Prime Minister opened a joint Norwegian-Danish embassy in Myanmar. Norway has supported the movement of democracy in the country for over decades. Given our long-term commitment, it was also important for us to act when we saw that a new time was on the rise. I myself visited Myanmar for the first time in April 2011 as a State Secretary. It was the first political visit of some Western government after the new powers had taken over after the dictator Than Shwe. I realized that it was a significant, almost surprising, willingness to change at the new authorities. When I was there later that same year, I saw that many changes had already occurred. And since 2011, the government of Myanmar has concluded truce agreements with ten of the 11 non-governmental armed groups. The vast majority of political prisoners are released. Aung San Suu Kyi has been elected to the parliament of the country and is head of the Committee on Legal Affairs and Citizens' Rights. Media censorship is almost gone, and in this area Myanmar is in fact far ahead of a number of countries in the region, including countries we have seen for many years as relatively democratic. A new investment law has been passed, and Myanmar has gradually gained a central role in ASEAN and takes over the presidency next year. At the same time, we should not underestimate the major challenges. The situation in the Kachin province is unclear. Although peace talks are going on right now, it's not long since there were strikes in the province. The violence that flared up between the peoples of the Rakhine province has also led to humanitarian suffering. We have reminded the authorities in clear order that they have a duty to protect everyone who lives in the kingdom. The country needs political processes that lead to lasting peace solutions, capacity building and inclusive economic growth. Following the lifting of the sanctions, Norway has been working to concrete the future Norwegian effort and commitment both in the country and in the region. In the field of peace, we started to support concrete projects in the arms rest areas in 2012. We took on the leadership of the donor group to coordinate support for these areas. The actors themselves are in the driver’s seat in the peace process, which is crucial. To conclude, in our changing world, in a time of breaking, we must protect our interests and our fundamental values. We should be aware of the shift of power and influence – economic, political and cultural – that is happening to the east, and we should promote our interests, not least in the north, and our well-established relations in the neighbourhoods, Europe, and the transatlantic ties. We should also be as prepared to face global security threats as possible. All of this requires an adapted foreign service and a clear strategic vision for Norwegian foreign policy.

DKK in tolls, 24 bn. SEK in interest, SEK 8.5 billion. NOK in collection and SEK 94,5 billion. kr in toll contributions to various projects. ParlaMint-NO_2013-02-12.ana.ud1094e1254	Avinor is currently responsible for both airports and air navigation services. Air navigation services are currently organised in a separate division. Through its Section 10 plan, delivered to the Ministry of Ownership, Avinor has proposed that the air navigation division be separated as a separate wholly-owned subsidiary. Furthermore, Avinor has an ambition to have a leading position in the North European airspace block, NEFAB, and any extensions thereof. If tower services were to be put out to competition in the future, the company also aims to be dominant in its domestic market. The Ministry of Transport, which owns it, is now considering the proposals submitted by the Board of Directors of Avinor. In this connection, a report has been prepared by the Danish consultancy company Integra. The Intega report shows that the issue of the outsourcing and external involvement of air navigation activities raises a number of questions that will need a more detailed investigation. The Ministry of Transport will come back to these issues as well as the issue of the separation of air navigation division in a separate subsidiary in the context of the Avinor ownership notification. However, the Ministry will point out that Norway has already undertaken international cooperation commitments in the field of en route services through the connection to the North European functional airspace block NEFAB. The Ministry further points out that we have a strong desire for closer Nordic cooperation for the en route services in the future, for example. through the integration of NEFAB and the Danish-Swedish airspace block. With regard to the situation in the air navigation service, the Board of Directors and the day-to-day management of Avinor are responsible for implementing measures necessary to ensure adequate robustness of air navigation services, i.e. in the provision of services. The Ministry of Transport follows this closely as the authority for aviation and as the manager of the State ownership of Avinor. As authority, the Ministry of Transport has designated Avinor as the national air navigation service provider. In connection with the extension of the Decision by the Ministry of Transport last autumn, i.e. in the autumn of 2012, it was also decided that from now on, the Air Safety Authority could ask Avinor to submit plans to ensure that the robustness of the service provision can be ensured and improved. In addition, the Air Safety Authority may now request detailed plans and explanations on how Avinor will ensure the manning of air traffic controllers. Furthermore, the Ministry of Transport introduced a new performance management system in 2012. The performance management system shall measure Avinor’s air navigation services in the areas of charging level and delays in addition to safety and the environment. The Ministry of Transport will otherwise indicate that the Air Safety Authority has recently conducted a hearing on a proposal that Norwegian knowledge be no longer required in order to be able to operate as an air traffic controller in a control centre. That matter is now under consideration by the Air Safety Authority. As a result of the Common Customs Union, the general rule within the EU is that customs handling is to take place at the final destination aerodrome, while EU security legislation ensures that the passenger does not need to undergo security controls more than once during an flight. Sweden and Finland have been exempted from this general rule and have introduced a simplified customs treatment system in Stockholm and Helsinki without the need for a new security check. Simplified customs treatment for passengers coming from abroad, which will continue with domestic flights in Norway, will also be a positive measure for Norwegian travellers. For Norway, the case raises some difficult issues that need to be resolved, not least the possibility of the customs tariff maintaining border controls at the current level as well as the economic consequences of different options for simplified transfers. The Ministry of Transport has therefore, in consultation with the Ministry of Finance, set up a wide-ranging working group to investigate this. The group has representatives from the aviation industry, police and customs authorities and is asked to consider different options for changes to the current system. The final report of the working group is not yet available, but the government will decide on the recommendation of the working group as soon as the report is available.

After ten years of neglect – or ten years of neglect – I will correct it – of the maintenance of the Norwegian railway system, it was the closest to broken back when we took over almost eight years ago. We've done something about that. Since then, the appropriations for the railway works have doubled and tripled, and we are going to develop it further. The development is reversed: the trains are almost on track, several take the train, there are new trains in place and more and more customers are satisfied with NSB – that’s good news. Collective transport, cycling and walking are obviously the best solutions in cities, while roads, planes and ferries will be the main solution across the country. We have to think more thoughts at once. The railway is and will be important in and around the big cities to transport people to and from work. Shorter planning time is something we're all concerned about, and there's a work going on that I have great expectations that we'll see a new model for when we get to the National Transport Plan. As I said, much is done, and even more is going to be in place. The centre party is concerned that we should be able to get a bet that will benefit the whole country in the form of increased good, collective solutions in the city, but also a bet that takes care of the value creation and growth that we want across the country.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-02-12.ana.ud1094e1845	Work on establishing a robust, common emergency network based on modern technology is an important contribution to social security and enhanced preparedness in Norway. Emergency networks are being developed to meet the needs defined by the emergency budgets. The development takes place in line with the frameworks of the Stortinget and the recommendation of the external quality insurer, including the Stortingen's assumptions on quality and surface coverage requirements. The government sees it as a priority task to ensure that national emergency networks come into place by the end of 2015 and within the framework of 4.7 billion. kr and to ensure that emergency networks are operated as a safe and efficient tool. Emergency networks are in daily use throughout the first stage of development. The second stage of development is now under way with many activities at the same time. Equipment for emergency rooms is provided and the Emergency Communications Directorate informs that the installation of the emergency rooms is in line with the plan and that seven new control rooms were already in use in 2012. Base stations are being built too fully in East and South Norway, and radio planning for the whole country is under way. Base stations and control rooms are connected by means of a so-called core network. A core network is basically large computers that make up the brain of the Emergency Network – a kind of central, in other words. The current core network is dimensioned for the first stage of development, and now we need a larger and safer core network that can serve all of Norway. This new core network is installed and being tested. Afterwards, all base stations from the first stage of construction and then all new base stations shall be connected to this core network. The switchover must be rapid and nearly risk-free and with minimum service interruptions. In view of the important and sometimes life-critical effects of emergency situations, one will not switch to a new solution until this has been adequately tested. I mention this because this is a technologically demanding project. The switchover was planned to be completed in the spring of 2013. In order to minimise the risk associated with the switchover, the plans must take into account the relocation after the summer holidays. The transfer of users is planned in close cooperation with the emergency budgets, which also have a number of specific activities related to this. The time when Emergency Networks are ready to be deployed in Hedmark, West Oppland and Gudbrandsdalen is therefore shifted to the fourth quarter of 2013. Land-wide development plans by 2015 are not affected by this. Feedback has been received from some users on challenges related to the use of Emergency Networks. In particular, this coverage is for indoor floors operating in the Oslo Centre. These feedback is taken seriously and the Emergency Communications Directorate has a close dialogue with the emergency services on improvement activities in many areas. Among other things, there is a project for measuring indoor coverage in about 100 major constructions in the first extension area. A quality review of the network has been started. In addition, information activities are ongoing to ensure that knowledge of what is and what is not is increased throughout the country. The emergency network project is, in my opinion, well-informed and also firmly anchored in Stortinget. The current year has been reported by the Emergency Network and the status of the case in Meld. St. 29 for 2011-2012, Social security. This is also mentioned in Prop. 1 S for 2012–2013 and in Prop. 23 S for 2012–2013. Furthermore, a number of written questions have been answered by representatives and, as mentioned earlier today, also during the examination of the interpellation of Mr Ingerd Schou on Emergency Network in Stortinget on 13 November last year. In the announcement to the Stortinget about the follow-up of the NOU 2012:14, Report of the 22nd July Commission, I want to give an updated review of, among other things. progress in development, budget and achievement. It is on this basis that I have not seen the need for a separate announcement to the Stortinget on Emergency Nets in the spring session this year.
