ParlaMint-NO_2013-02-12.ana.ud1094e2001	No more people have asked for the floor on case No. 7th@item: inlistbox
ParlaMint-NO_2013-02-12.ana.ud1094e2017	It is not uncommon in either interpellation debates or in other debates here in the Chamber that some of the purpose of lifting the case is to show that there is a difference between parties and blocks in the Storting. That is not the intention today; I would rather say the opposite. The background to my delivery of this interview is the desire to have a debate that can provide a basis for a common understanding and expectation of the concept of a people's church. This I do naturally enough because I have experienced that the term is used very differently, often unyanished and often taken to income for an understanding that I myself cannot see related to what the Right has founded on the concept. Another point is that it may be natural that this magnification also takes up a debate on the concept of ‘the Church of People’. It's been a while since the state-church agreement and the debate that was then. Now it is as the concept introduced in the Constitution and deserves attention. It can be natural to start with what I see as the content of the term. I perceive that the concept of a people's church is used to give the Norwegian Church a continued place in society as an important and present institution for the Norwegian people, as it has been for a long time. While equal treatment of religious and life-seeking societies has been clarified by changes in the Constitution and the Law of the Church, it is still established that the Norwegian Church has a special position in our society. I think I share this view with most parts of the political environment. I also think we have a common understanding and a wish that this means that we believe the Church should be present for the people throughout the country, and that they should set up in a special way with their competences of humanity and care when society is affected by events that require extra effort by the community. I have correctly perceived some critical voices that respond to the Church being so closely connected through both national and municipal crisis management, but I understand that at the same time we believe that the experience and competence they possess is a contribution we cannot fail to use. So there is a small challenge in the desire for an advocacy church that stands up for the people, and the preservation and respect of those who do not want to be met by the Church in such challenging situations. I think there are many good, practical arrangements for how to solve this, and have great faith in local solutions to it. So far, I feel that there are no major challenges in a common church understanding. So to what's more of a concern to me. I have registered a number of statements that, in my opinion, interfere with how the Church should handle matters that are directly linked to theology and learning questions. I hear statements that the Church, because it is to be a people's church, must be generous and tolerant, and not push people away. Yes, it's not difficult to share the desire for a raw, inclusive and open church, but if we believe with this that the Church should unpack controversial theological questions, or leave aside to present doctrinal views from which some might be provoked, we draw the popular church concept too far. The purpose of the changes we have made was that we as a state should no longer govern the Church's inner life, and that the Church should conduct its business in the pact with its faith and learning without any interference from the state. Just the right to operate their religious and life - vision organization based on one's own conviction, and the freedom of the individual's right to religion, have been strengthened by the changes we made on 21 July 2012. My people's church should be clear about what it stands for, and not hide it. So on this basis it's going to be both raw, inclusive and open. I hope, however, that in this debate we can agree that there should be a delineation of the popular church concept against theology and learning questions. It's positive for the Church to be a church for all the people, but it'll be going across the border if we expect everyone to be able to define themselves within. It was a little bit about what I think the church shouldn't be – now a little back to what we think it can be. I will return to the physical presence throughout the country, but will promise another presence that is also important. We have a long and good church tradition in our country, and an important part of this is the Church as the traveler of the value debate. Should that not also be an important task for the Church in the future? What values are challenged, what values are at risk of losing their place in society, and what values must be brought into new debates? I hope it is, but is a little bit more uncertain about whether it is, a consensus that this is a clear task for the people's church. Of course, this task is also closely linked to the Church's task as a cultural bearer, and not least the Church as part of a greater reality, an international church with its commitment far beyond preaching and sacrament. The Church's commitment as part of a welfare offer both nationally and internationally has a natural place in a people's church. So back to presence. Our task as politicians is to facilitate the Church to carry out its mission as a religious community and a church of the people. In this context, it is very important to think through what is going on. When we say that the Church should be present throughout the country, we also say that there should be work, which requires both human and financial resources. It's great that we do, but it requires that we follow up, and that we give clear feedback to the Church about what we expect. What does presence mean? What about church buildings and other facilities, and what about the frequency and breadth of the offer? There is no doubt that here we have expectations of the Church, which is extensive and, in part, precious. It is probably also here that it is clear that there are quite different expectations of the religious community The Norwegian Church than of the other religious and life-view societies, that the Church of Nations has a expectation that the others do not. It takes wisdom to manage this in a good way, both in the Church and with us politicians. In my opinion, the expectation of the people's Church along with non-legislative treatment is two lines that are in the process of crossing each other, and it can quickly be seen as a collision course. A natural question to ask is therefore whether the Church of the People, the Church of the Majority in Norway, should nevertheless be discriminated against in certain areas. The expectations themselves are, of course, discriminatory. “I prefer to ask, my call is not to answer” – yes, it’s tempting to hang on to Ibsen now. However, I think there is expected to be a little hint of a response to this too, and I dare to vent a thought, even if it is not a final conclusion. Not long ago, religious and life-view societies experienced a change in the grant to them as a result of a change in the way foreign members of the Norwegian Church were counted. It seems unreasonable, and it was very little predictable. Perhaps it is time to put in place a more robust system for calculating grants and non-discrimination between these organisations. Maybe a look at some tasks the Church of the People, or, for that matter, other religious and life views, does for society and gives them resources for it, without that being part of the contribution we all make, depending on the number of members. I think at least it's necessary to go through this thoroughly and find a good solution to how one can treat organisations that are discriminated against in expectations and tasks. Finally, I hope that this will not be a debate on economics. Once I have also raised that part of the debate, it is to show that we have legally introduced a concept that challenges us in practical follow-up. The most important thing, however, has been the desire for a debate that can confirm that we have a common desire, a common understanding that we want a people's church to be present throughout the country, a people's church that is to be part of an offer to the people in everyday life and crisis, which is to be raw, inclusive and open, but which is to deal entirely with its inner life and learning questions itself, and have the right to express its views with boldness. ParlaMint-NO_2013-02-12.ana.ud1094e2045	First, I would like to thank the representative Harberg for the interpellation and the question of what is contained in the term ‘people’s church’. I see it as an invitation to a debate on the overall objectives of church policy. It is a conversation I would like to welcome, and in which I would like to participate. When the parties at Stortinget gathered on 10 April 2008 about the church settlement, it provided a basis for amending the Basic Law provisions on the state church system. The term ‘public church’ came for the first time into the Constitution. I perceive the wording of the new provision in the Basic Law § 16 that «The Norwegian Church remains the Church of Norway», primarily as an expression of the Storting’s will for continuity in the area of the Church. [[...]] The church agreement implies that changes were made that strengthened the independence of the Norwegian Church as a religious community. At the same time, the church agreement showed that there is a broad political uprising to take care of «our Christian and humanistic heritage». And there was agreement to facilitate that the Norwegian Church could be a church in which the vast majority of the nation's population knows each other. There is still reason to move forward step by step in terms of changes in the Church's arrangements. It touches matters that are deeply involved in many members of the Church. Excessive and rapid changes can lead to long-term consequences that are difficult to understand. That's why continuity is important. People should not experience that the Church is being taken from them. How the Norwegian Church appears and understands itself as a majority church in today's multicultural and multireligious societies is a theological question. The Church, through its elected bodies and theological teaching offices, has an independent responsibility to develop and express. In respect for the Church as a religious community, a state church policy will not have the aim of deciding this. Our task as state authority is to facilitate economic, legal and organisational conditions so that the Norwegian Church can ‘remain’ the Church of Norway. In this, there is an objective that the Church's activities remain nationwide. It shall be available in the individual communities, with services and services and with the possibility for members of the Church to be baptized and confirmed, dedicated and buried. As a popular church, the Norwegian Church will meet with a special expectation that it will embrace the many. It must give room for different theological directions within its recognition. There must be room for different degrees of religious commitment. The Church must have the ability to see diversity as a enrichment, not as a threat. It must have the will to be liberal and to embrace it widely. Should future generations be able to know membership of the Church, knowledge of the Church's faith and values must be communicated to them. That is why the reform of faith training is important. It's a popular church project. The reform's objective of reaching the breadth of its members is important. Therefore, training provision needs to be designed in such a way that it is felt to be relevant to most people. It is a major challenge for the Norwegian Church to facilitate a broader education of faith than is currently the case. The Church shall be experienced as close, relevant and accessible to the 77 pst. of the people of Norway who belong to it, the members of the Church must express themselves, take responsibility for them and make decisions about their church. That is why the democratic reform envisaged by the church settlement is also a popular church project. During the spring session, the government will present proposals for changes to the church law provisions on elections, as requested by the Stortinget. At the same time, the Church Council is working to prepare for the Church meeting to establish the more detailed electoral system. The purpose of these changes is to ensure the prerequisites of the church settlement. There it said that real choices should be established, that one should have increased use of direct elections, and that church elections should take place at the same time as public elections. I expect that the Church will not end the democratic reform once the electoral system has been adopted. Developing democratic structures and cultures with transparency and respect for different views is an ever-increasing challenge. The historical premise and the special position of the Norwegian Church in the Norwegian community provide the basis for its own form of funding and for a more comprehensive regulation of the law of this church than that of other religious and life-view societies. As a majority church with a large majority of the population as members, it is not unnatural that the Norwegian Church performs social tasks such as: tomb management and alerting of deaths on police mission. The Steelset Committee, like several previous committees of inquiry, has, on a principle basis, been in favour of municipalities being responsible for the management of the burial site. In previous rounds, the hearings have shown great support for continued church management of the graveyards, and I believe initially that today's system still works well. The church agreement also assumes that today's arrangement will continue. The Norwegian Church will continue to play an important, gathering role at festivals and major events, in sorrow as in joy. There is a broad political basis for continuing such practices within the framework of freedom of belief. I believe that the church agreement and the new provisions of the Constitution confirm this. The amendments to the Constitution have provided a new basis for further independence of the Norwegian Church. As also mentioned in the 2013 budget proposal, the consideration of continuity must continue to weigh heavily in the further development of the Church's framework and arrangements. Further changes should take place step by step and have broad political and church uprisings. The Church Council is actively examining proposals for further administrative reforms in the Church. I believe it is natural now to await the proposals adopted by the Church's bodies. However, the Ministry has taken the initiative to evaluate the system established for the local church in the 1996 Church Law Reform. There is a need for more knowledge of what is in practice the main challenges of the local church organisation. The organisation, management and activities of the individual parish are of the utmost importance. It's local that people meet their church. That's above all that's the people's church. Finally, I would like to stress that the popular church concept does not, of course, mean that only the church members belong to the community, or that you have to be a member of the Norwegian Church in order to be Norwegian. It's a derailment. The Norwegian Church, with its 3.8 million members, has a separate role in Norwegian society. It has represented the Christian cultural heritage that has shaped the country over a thousand years. As a large majority church, it has a special responsibility. This constitutes, as I see it, a factual basis for the fact that in certain areas there are specific frameworks and conditions for the Norwegian Church. This is how the Norwegian Church can be the Church of Norway and its members can still be associated with this church.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-02-12.ana.ud1094e2304	The non-profit voluntary organisations constitute a valuable and, in particular, a contribution to the welfare society, in relation to both public operations and commercial activities. Many experience the meeting with the organisation as the rescue of a difficult life situation or as an offer they have missed in the public utility. An example is the Court of Justice, which is a user-led foundation that addresses its offer to the long-term abuse of alcohol and drugs, as well as to repeaters in the criminal justice system. The Court meets users as equals and can continue its own experience in such a way that drug addicts, criminals or relatives can make better use of a treatment offer within and outside institutions. It was after the committee meeting with this organisation earlier this year that I took the initiative to take a debate. When organisations meet politicians, it is often for advice on where to apply and how to deal with the public. It's actually very difficult to answer, even for us as the granting authority. It's quite inconclusive. The entitlement is only one of several who find it difficult to compete with large, well-known and well-established organisations for the same grant items. They do not have the administrative resources required to reach them. In bidding rounds, they do not come because they cannot deliver everything they need, and the large organisations naturally do not want subcontractors as long as they can do the job themselves. If these organisations are to adapt to the tendering system, the specific nature of the tender will disappear. The requirement of formal competence, different positions, etc. does not always fit into the concepts of the various organisations based on ideology, and not on the offer normally made by public authorities. Organisations based initially on non-profit, or based on equal work, which fill an empty space in the public utility must invest more and more resources to provide resources for operations. This is very demanding for organisations with relatively few employees and small administrative appliances. This also takes valuable time and money from what should have been used to provide a good welfare offer, and not from lobbying or administrative tasks to get involved in the youth of grant schemes. It is assumed that lobbying has increased considerably in recent years, but there is no basis for numerical developments. The growth of an industry such as carrying out and assisting in lobbying can be an indication that lobbying is increasing to an extent, and this offer can itself contribute to creating demand. It is difficult to imagine anything other than that such recruitment will result in an increase in work tasks related to lobbying against the public sector. The cost of lobbying, in particular if organised and with professional assistance, is easier to bear for economically strong organisations than for others. For this reason, lobbying can be criticised for creating inequalities in access to democratic decision-making processes. Voluntary and non-profit institutions serving households may act as a public policy instrument. As ‘instruments’, the grants that contribute to the achievement of policy objectives and values are received, but the special thing is that they develop offers that cannot be replaced by the public. In the context of new public health law, the government has said that it will follow up the work of systematically and actively including NPISHs in the further work on the cooperation reform. I ask, what has happened, and how does the government think to follow up on this? Such a system is currently particularly difficult for new actors developing new offers, to continue or further develop their offers when predictability is lacking. Ideal and voluntary work starts with collected funds and fires, but requires permanent paid staff as tasks increase. This applies not only to small, new actors, but also to well-known, large organisations that have previously been based mainly on voluntary efforts. Society is developing and changes are also relevant to the operation of voluntary work and non-profit institutions serving households. It is important that both the well-established, the large organisations and the smaller and the newer are given the opportunity to contribute to the fulfilment of the intentions of big messages and the laws that the Stortinge adopts – I mention, among other things. Cooperation reform and the Public Health Act. The volunteers contribute to important areas of drugs, psychiatry, poverty, to criminals, children, disabled people, the elderly, and I will also mention the animal protection organisations. There have been many examples of the news in recent times that have shown that there is a need for much closer cooperation with voluntary organisations towards the public sector. Interaction and cooperation are important, but what is also important is that it does not build up a large bureaucracy that takes money. There should be a transparent system that is easily accessible. There is broad political agreement on the important role of organisations in the future as a complement to public services, but they need better conditions. Organisations cannot live without public contributions. The ambition for all to contribute to the working life of their workforce is also cross-political, and if this ambition is fulfilled, many who are currently contributing to voluntary unpaid work will be in paid jobs. What I call for in this debate is a better overall overview of all grant schemes and how the funds are distributed. More predictability and simpler application processes are also needed which do not create unnecessary bureaucracy for organisations. It is good social economy to organize for the ideal and voluntary sector, and as I see it, unnecessary bureaucracy is wasted welfare.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-02-12.ana.ud1094e2332	The representative of Giltun asks whether the government will facilitate and coordinate the government grant schemes. I assume that there are grant schemes that are important to the non-profit organisations, the representative asks me to respond to. Let me first stress that the investment in voluntary and non-profit institutions is greater under this government than ever before. One of the main sources of funding for the activity of the non-profit organisations is not grants, but revenues from the provision of welfare services to the public. The Government has entered into a cooperation agreement with the umbrella organisations Freedom Norway, the Main Organisation Work, the Ideal Network and the KS Enterprise, representing non-profit actors providing health and social services to state activities. The agreement recognises that the non-profit sector is an important public sector partner in the provision of health and social services to the population and shall contribute to better cooperation between the state and the non-profit sector. The aim of the agreement is to further develop and improve the service provision of patients and users. It is my department that has worked on this agreement, and as the one responsible for its follow-up, I am happy to be able to participate in the debate here today. This also applies because the proposal is about simplification in the public sector, as I am also responsible for the government's work on this. The government has strengthened the framework conditions of non-governmental organisations by establishing and stepping up the VAT compensation scheme for non-governmental organisations. The scheme was established in January 2010 with a budget of 396 million. kr. In 2012, around 16 000 associations and layers received VAT compensation. In 2013, the allocation to the scheme is 948 million. kr. The government has established a voluntary register and a collection register. The Ministry of Culture has launched a three-year project in the register field to ensure that data reporting is as simple as possible for organisations. The records and data from the registers shall also be optimally used by the administration and others. Work is carried out in consultation with the voluntary sector. The research programme ‘Virtual Centre for Research in Civil Society and the Voluntary Sector’ was launched in 2008 and has contributed to knowledge of changes and developments in the voluntary sector. 135 new volunteer centres have been established since 2005. We've strengthened the Free Fund scheme by about 50 million. NOK over the State budget, in addition to an increase in the share of the scheme over the key. In the case of more money that can be used freely for their own purposes, the government has made it easier for the voluntary sector in recent years. At the same time, the sector has indicated that application and reporting requirements are perceived as bureaucratic. They may also find it difficult to get an overview of all that is possible to apply for. The government has a clear objective that organisations should spend minimal time on forms and maximum time on activity. I Prop. No 1 S for 2012–2013: ‘The government will facilitate interaction between the public and voluntary sectors. The aim is to facilitate, where appropriate, application and reporting requirements relating to different State grant schemes and to facilitate the availability of information on State grant schemes.’;» At the request of the Committee on Family Affairs and Culture of the United Kingdom, the Government, in its work on simplification and coordination of grant schemes, has in the first instance concentrated on the organisations of children and young people. The Ministry of Culture has established a website with an overview of grants that children’s and youth organisations can apply for. A permanent meeting point has been established between state support managers, children’s and youth organisations and the Ministry to ensure a sustained focus on the coordination and simplification of grant schemes. Work has been started on developing an adviser for managers of grants to children’s and youth organisations. The Ministry of Culture is currently carrying out a survey of all State grant schemes for which the entire voluntary sector can apply. This will provide valuable knowledge for further work on assessing more simplification and coordination measures for grant schemes. It is a good intention of the representative Giltun to create a website with a full overview of government grant schemes. But we have to ask ourselves how it is most appropriate to gather such information. We have already established a website with an overview of grants that children’s and youth organisations can apply for. This makes sense because in many ways there is a uniform candidate group with member-based organisations. In addition, there are very high levels of management tournaments in the children’s and youth organisations. It makes it extra important to organise with good information from the state. If we are to go further in the direction of a common portal for all state grant schemes, it will become significantly more complicated. Among other things, it will have to adapt to applicants who are very different both in terms of organisation and the purpose of the organisation. We need to consider the whole thing, both what can make it easier for those seeking, and not least the administrative and economic consequences of different proposals. I see that it could be useful with more information about the needs of the non-profit organisations before we take a final position on the proposal for a common portal. I would therefore like to take the initiative to discuss this issue with the organisations in connection with one of the dialogue meetings to which the cooperation agreement refers. Norway needs the ideal organisations. The government will be a partner in contributing to a strong and living civil society.

However, the picture is not quite clear, and there are good examples of enterprises such as the Netherlands, Germany and the United Kingdom. fast-food chains and retail chains in Denmark and Sweden are linked to developmentally impaired staff. We also see examples of this in Norway. Better follow-up and awareness-raising among Norwegian employers could lead to a change in attitude and thus increase participation of the developmentally handicapped in the ordinary labour market. In the research report «Integrated objectives or broken visions?» by senior researcher Sylvia Söderström and Professor Jan Tøssebro at NTNU, it appears that the proportion of people with developmental disabilities in the measure Permanently organised work was 30 p.m. in 2001, while in 2010 it had sunk to 13 p.m. by employees. If there is something the Workers' Party is satisfied with, the Advance Party cannot agree to that. There is no strengthening of the ability of those with disabilities to have a job. Daycare is not a satisfactory solution, and it is quite frightening how many people with developmental disabilities go directly from school to stay at home. They wonder why they've gone to school for 13-14 years if they're not going to be able to get anything to do. VTA companies have had and will continue to have an important role as permanent jobs for those who cannot work in the ordinary enterprises, but also as a link between ordinary employers and employees with a developmental disability. Employees of the country’s VTA companies have both top-notch competence and good and long tradition of guiding and facilitating the proper functioning of those with developmental disabilities in the workplace. The potential for development is therefore high for VTA companies to act as development platforms with a clear objective of maximising the normal labour market. The Progress Party believes that one of the main factors in facilitating transition from school to work is the use of individual plans. We are aware that the Ministry of Knowledge recently consulted on the proposal for the provision of individual levels in the Training Act, and expect that the consultation recordings that further strengthen legislation will be followed. We also believe it is necessary to improve understanding of what the plans are to contain and how they are to be implemented. Persons with developmental disabilities must be given equal opportunities, in line with others, for training that reflects their interests and facilities. Therefore, more attention should be paid to appropriate, qualified training that strengthens students’ own facilities and interests, in view of job opportunities. In the proposal, we have raised many important points to improve the transition from school to work. We hope that the government will take it with them when they now prepare an overall plan. We also believe that the teacher’s candidate system, which contributes to providing students with vocational competence through practical work, should be strengthened, and see that there may be problems with Nav funding the actions. The Progress Party also sees that people with disabilities and developmental disabilities will enjoy working in companies where they are co-owners themselves. I want to refer there to the Erlandsen pastoral in Drammen, which opened in August 2012 – I visited them on the opening day – where developmentally disabled people have started their own business where they are joint owners. It is now apparent that there are no measures and aid schemes that help to start own enterprises by those with disabilities. I hope that the government will also take it with them. There is a cross-political consensus that it is important that the right to work is respected by those with disabilities, but the Progress Party cannot see that the government's work strategy for people with disabilities is adequately addressed. Most have experienced being at a workplace where people with developmental disabilities show great joy and pride in their work. Our goal is to give everyone the right to work if they can and will, and most people can and will. I take up the proposal of the Progress Party and the Christian People's Party and report that we will vote in a subsidiary way for the Right's proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-02-12.ana.ud1094e3002	In this case, it is not important to stand and promote self-confidence and satisfaction over a situation that everyone knows is not as it should be. Many of them have very good finances, but they don't have the ability to create multiple places, because Nav won't pay. The municipality is responsible for the daily supply. It's a shared responsibility that's politically desirable, and there are also these users becoming throwballs in the system. In the case of people with developmental disabilities, they are a very special group, usually born with a disability, which means that they cannot seek ordinary work in the same way as everyone else. They depend entirely on public support to enter a job, whether it is a private job or there is some permanent work that the municipality has. That's what I also want rights to. When a student leaves school, it is known that there must be support schemes to help the person in work. If the person does not stay at home or ends up on a daily offer where nothing very productive happens. It is completely wrong to use funds to place young people on a daily offer instead of having them, after completing a school, go to work. It is this that needs to be improved by planning early, and looking at the abilities and interests of individuals, in order to guide the student towards getting as organized a job as possible. Then it doesn't help to say in this room that we have increased the number of spaces when the need is increasing and when more and more people sit at home and get nothing to do. Why should they complete a school run when they are placed at home for years afterwards, and at the same time know that there is a job in the municipality or somewhere else where they could be involved and contribute if only the public had come with funds. I wish it was automatic in that the funds followed these users so that they got straight to work when it was possible and desirable.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-02-12.ana.ud1094e3075	The draft representative we are here to debate, on a new coal strategy, addresses issues that this government has had on the agenda over almost two major periods and that have been followed up by active policies and concrete actions. One of the truly far-reaching health reforms in recent times is the Interaction Reform. It is a policy reform that has put public health, not least, on the top political agenda, where prevention and health promotion are at the centre of attention. The interaction reform has highlighted the major public diseases of the chronic non-infectious diseases category, which can be largely prevented by lifestyle changes such as smoking, reduced consumption of sugar and fat, more moderate alcohol consumption and by being more physically active. Cols is one of the most serious diseases that has proven smoking as its main cause. Both coal and other chronic lung diseases affect a large group of people. The number of coal diseases in Norway is about. 370,000 and increases by 20,000 per year. At ca. 70 pst. of patients, tobacco smoking is a causal factor, while other cases are related to professional relationships — in or without combination with tobacco. It was to meet this reality that in 2006 the government launched the National Strategy for the COLS region 2006–2011 and established the National COLS Council as part of the strategy. This strategy has been a necessary and useful tool in the fight against the carbon epidemic of which we see the contours. The United Nations and the World Health Organisation also have direct attention to coal as a very serious disease. The government's carbon strategy includes, inter alia: the establishment of a national quality register for persons with coal — in operation from 2011 — and national guidelines and guidelines for the prevention, diagnosis and follow-up of coal have been published. In letters to the Health and Care Committee of 14 December 2012, the Council of State points out that the further efforts to prevent coal should be integrated into the cross-sectoral public health work covered by the Public Health Act and in the follow-up to the Cooperation Reform. In this context, it is emphasised that the Interaction Reform focuses precisely on better and more coordinated treatment and rehabilitation services. And more carbon atoms can get offers through e.g. Intermediary units, in the island assistance services provided by municipalities or in their own homes, e.g. by means of new medical technology. In 2011 it is granted 30 million. NOK for the treatment and rehabilitation of chroniclers in municipalities. In several places in the country, pilot projects are being carried out to test patients with carbon blacks at home. The equipment provides a two-way communication with sound and image between medical personnel and patients, and the device is intended to provide increased safety for patients by improving the overview of e.g.: cardiac and pulmonary function and by adjusting treatment measures. The goal is to improve the pathology and thus increase the patient’s ability to live at home. This measure will also be evaluated. The long-term primary prevention must aim at reducing the incidence of carbon. This should primarily be done by seeking to reduce tobacco smoking significantly and by actively combating harmful inhalation into the labour market. Early diagnosis of coal is important and allows for the earliest possible intervention through smoking cessation and lifestyle change. A very positive result of the carbon strategy is that the number of daily smokers in the population is falling, and that this is happening particularly among the youngest. I am pleased that this government has a great deal of attention to public health work through prevention and health promotion, and I look forward to the public health message coming in ours. I think it is right to see the work to reduce the major public diseases — including carbon — as an integral part of the broad public health strategy that we are now going to have, and not as specific measures that require specific, specific strategies.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-02-12.ana.ud1094e3396	The abortion law currently has rules to balance the consideration of the pregnant woman on the one hand and the consideration of the foetus on the other. Before the end of the 12th week of pregnancy, the attention of the woman is heavier. Then she decides whether or not to interrupt pregnancy. In addition to pregnancy, more and more attention is paid to the legal protection of the foetus. After 12 weeks, abortion students decide whether or not pregnancy can be interrupted. If pregnancy has reached the age of 18 weeks, particularly severe reasons must be given to permit abortions. If there is reason to believe that the foetus is viable, interruptions may only be made if there is an imminent risk to the life or health of the pregnant woman. A fetus is considered viable if it is able to survive outside the mother's life at the time of the interruption. In that assessment, one shall take into account the possibilities for treatment after any redemption. In order to avoid viable abortions, the Christian People's Party proposes that abortions after the 18th week of pregnancy should be allowed only if there is an imminent risk to the woman's life or health or the foetus has a condition incompatible with life. I cannot support this proposal. In my opinion, it is not necessary to amend the law in order to ensure the legal protection of the foetus. Abortion of viable foetuses shall not occur unless pregnancy poses an imminent risk to the life and health of the pregnant woman. If the harm to the foetus is removed as an independent basis for abortion after the end of the 12th week of pregnancy, I believe that the women have to do just that. I believe that serious harm to the foetus should be maintained as an independent basis for granting abortion between the 12th and the 18th week of pregnancy.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-02-12.ana.ud1094e3486	The abortion law in Norway is based on the notion that the human dignity of the foetus is increasing with age. Before the 12th week, no legal protection has been imposed in Norwegian law. The time 12 weeks is quite random. There are no medical or biological events in the foetus during this week that indicate that the foetus will gain more value after 12 weeks. It's also the way that healthy fetuses gradually get legal protection. After 12 weeks, abortion may be granted if the foetus has anomalies or illness, or if the pregnancy, birth or care of the child can lead to undue stress on the physical or mental health of the woman. In this way, abortion legislation is based on a gradation of human life. When should a foetus be granted personal status and be subject to protection? This is a question that the Christian People's Party often addresses in difficult ethical debates. There are difficult balances to make every time we discuss these issues, but it is important for the Christian People's Party to be a voice that says something about all people being equally valuable, completely independent of what we are capable of performing, or whether we have diagnosis or deviation. This is important to us in the Christian People's Party. Children with disease and diagnosis are particularly vulnerable in their mother's stomach, and we are concerned that they should receive a vote and a reinforced legal protection. Therefore, there are three things that are important in this matter. One is the abortion limit up to 12 weeks, and there we disagree with the majority of the population. Since it is the owner of the instruments to manage critical risks associated with the overall framework for the conversion operation, this means that according to the National Audit Office’s assessment, ownership management has not been properly exercised within the formal framework of the Health Business Act.’;» The National Audit Office's report will be the subject of a separate case in Stortinget. Now we have the answer: Critical risk was reported up, responsibility was pushed down, although the means of doing something about what was reported in relation to critical risk were and was retained at the owner level. One now knows that the transfer from Oslo University Hospital to Aarhus was not well planned from the owner. That means that one of our latest hospitals, which has been planned for a long time, and where many of these assumptions have been known for a long time, has now become a hospital that employs a number of temporary, provisional solutions. The question that one should ask in such a situation is: When this was identified early in the process as the key risk factors, why does one end up having to use temporary solutions so late in the process, instead of using the identification of the risk to initiate action at an early stage, so that one of our latest hospitals actually appeared as a hospital dimensioned for the tasks it should have, rather than a hospital that is characterized by temporary solutions? That's a central question. The second key question I hope to be answered today is: How will the recognition that the ownership management of the process was not good enough lead to changes in ownership management in the follow-up to the long-term effects of the failure to properly manage the process?
